============================================================\n\\section{Tokenizer and Truncation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:tokenizer_analysis}\n\nDeepSeek VOTA seed 42 (84.45\\%) is a training failure driven by tokenizer-induced truncation.\nRe-evaluation of the seed 42 checkpoint with the standard max\\_length=2048 yields 16\\% accuracy, confirming complete model collapse rather than marginal degradation.\nThe previously reported 84.45\\% was obtained with max\\_length=16384, which partially bypasses tokenizer truncation.\nSeeds 123 and 456, evaluated under identical conditions, achieve 99.16\\% and 99.33\\% respectively.\nThe DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus 1{,}929 for Qwen, causing 91.7\\% of DeepSeek VOTA training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window.\nThe training framework preserves the verdict label but truncates trace content, so the model trains with incomplete diagnostic information for the majority of samples.\nSeeds 123 and 456 (99.16\\% and 99.33\\%) demonstrate that DeepSeek can learn VOTA when LoRA initialization avoids the truncation-sensitive basin; the 2-seed mean (99.25\\%$\\pm$0.12) matches the cross-model VOTA range (98.74--99.54\\%).\n\nA post-hoc group analysis on seed 42 provides directional evidence: partitioning test samples by whether their training-set counterparts exceeded the context window, intact samples ($N{=}99$) achieve 100\\% accuracy while truncated samples ($N{=}1{,}091$) achieve 83.04\\% (95\\% CI [80.75\\%, 85.24\\%]; Fisher exact $p{<}.001$), with all 185 errors concentrated in the fail class (fail recall = 31.23\\%).\nPer-class analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:per_class}) confirms that seed 42 achieves 100\\% fail precision but only 45.43\\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \\texttt{pass}.\n\nSeed robustness is consistent across models: Qwen \\texttt{exception\\_only} produces identical 99.83\\% across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}0$), and DeepSeek achieves 99.39\\%$\\pm$0.10 (seeds 42/456: 99.33\\%, seed 123: 99.50\\%).\nBecause \\texttt{exception\\_only} test inputs contain 48.99\\% duplicates (607 unique of 1,190; \\S\\ref{sec:dataset}), CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials; under deduplication ($n{=}607$), Qwen's accuracy is 99.67\\% (Wilson CI [98.81, 99.91]).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Persistent Error Analysis}\n\\label{sec:irreducible}\n\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition with Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B misclassifies exactly 2 out of 1,190 test samples across all three seeds (42, 123, 456), with 100\\% overlap across seeds.\nBoth samples (IDs 901 and 905) share the following characteristics:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{Function:} \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} (HumanEval/95), a function that checks whether all dictionary keys are uniformly upper-case or lower-case strings.\n\\item \\textbf{Ground-truth label:} \\texttt{pass}.\n\\item \\textbf{Predicted label:} \\texttt{fail} (false negative).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-seed consistency:} Both samples are misclassified identically by all three seeds, indicating a systematic rather than stochastic error.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nManual inspection reveals the root cause: \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} uses \\texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \\texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \\texttt{True} value, raising \\texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.\nBecause \\texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \\texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines, even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.\nThe model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \\texttt{fail}.\nThis is not a model failure but a representation ambiguity: the \\texttt{exception\\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.\n\nMost configurations converge to these same 2 errors: Qwen-1.5B, Qwen-7B (3 seeds), CodeLlama-13B (seeds 42/456), and DeepSeek-6.7B (seeds 42/456) all produce error set $\\{901, 905\\}$.\nHowever, CodeLlama-13B seed 123 achieves 100\\% accuracy (0/1190 errors), correctly classifying both samples.\nThis demonstrates that 99.83\\% is a near-perfect \\emph{floor} (the convergence attractor for most LoRA initializations) rather than a hard data ceiling: the representation supports perfect accuracy, but most initializations converge to the same 2-error basin.\nThe practical implication is that these errors are high-probability convergence artifacts addressable through seed selection or ensemble methods, not fundamental limitations of the exception-based representation.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Exception Type Accuracy Breakdown}\n\\label{sec:exception_type}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:exception_type} reports \\texttt{exception\\_only} accuracy stratified by exception type on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$).\nAll standard exception types (\\texttt{TypeError}, \\texttt{ValueError}, \\texttt{IndexError}, etc.) achieve 100\\% accuracy.\nThe only misclassifications arise from \\texttt{GeneratorExit}, a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass that is not an \\texttt{Exception}. It is raised by Python's iterator protocol during generator short-circuiting, not by actual program errors.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Exception type accuracy breakdown for \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Qwen-7B, seed=42). All true error types achieve 100\\%. The only failures come from \\texttt{GeneratorExit} (a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass, not an \\texttt{Exception}), which appears in 2 passing samples.}\n\\label{tab:exception_type}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Exception Type} & \\textbf{Samples} & \\textbf{Accuracy} \\\\\n\\midrule\nTypeError & 96 & 100\\% \\\\\nValueError & 62 & 100\\% \\\\\nIndexError & 41 & 100\\% \\\\\nKeyError & 28 & 100\\% \\\\\nAttributeError & 19 & 100\\% \\\\\nAssertionError & 15 & 100\\% \\\\\nRecursionError & 12 & 100\\% \\\\\nOther Exception & 66 & 100\\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nGeneratorExit (BaseException) & 2 & 0\\% \\\\\nNo exception (pass class) & 849 & 100\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThis confirms that the model has learned a clean decision boundary: any true \\texttt{Exception} subclass in the trace implies failure.\nThe \\texttt{GeneratorExit} case represents a semantic ambiguity in the trace format rather than a model failure, as discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:irreducible}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Trace Length Ablation}\n\\label{sec:trace_length_full}\n\nTo test whether the \\texttt{full\\_trace} performance gap is caused by input truncation at training time, we vary the maximum trace token budget on Qwen-2.5-7B (seed=42) while keeping all other hyperparameters fixed.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Trace length and truncation direction ablation on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). Head = standard left-truncation (retain first $k$ tokens); Tail = retain last $k$ tokens. Per-condition bootstrap 95\\% CIs ($B{=}10{,}000$).}\n\\label{tab:trace_length}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Direction} & \\textbf{Max Tokens} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nHead & 256  & 78.82 & [76.4, 81.0] \\\\\nHead & 512  & 80.42 & [78.2, 82.6] \\\\\nHead & 1024 & 77.23 & [74.9, 79.6] \\\\\nHead & no\\_limit ($\\infty$) & 81.76 & [79.7, 83.9] \\\\\n\\midrule\nTail & 256  & 77.48 & --- \\\\\nTail & 512  & 93.61 & --- \\\\\nTail & 1024 & 96.47 & --- \\\\\nTail & 2048 & 97.31 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{budget_curve}\n\\caption{Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$, four models, three architecture families). All models remain near baseline at tail-256/384, then jump +13.2--22.0pp at 384$\\to$512, converging into a 93.6--98.2\\% band by tail-1024. The shaded region marks the transition zone (${\\sim}$400--500 tokens) where structured execution markers become visible beyond the globals module dump. Dashed line: \\texttt{exception\\_only} range (99.3--99.9\\%); dotted line: \\texttt{no\\_trace} range (67--77\\%).}\n\\label{fig:trace_length}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAs shown in Table~\\ref{tab:trace_length} and Figure~\\ref{fig:trace_length}, all four trace length conditions fall within a 5pp range (77.2\\%--81.8\\%).\nTo quantify the reliability of the observed differences, Table~\\ref{tab:trace_length_pairwise} reports paired bootstrap comparisons ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42) across all condition pairs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Pairwise paired bootstrap comparisons for trace length ablation ($B{=}10{,}000$, seed=42). $\\Delta$ = accuracy difference between the two conditions (first $-$ second in the budget group; condition $-$ baseline in the other groups). **$p{<}.01$; ***$p{<}.001$.}\n\\label{tab:trace_length_pairwise}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\textbf{Comparison} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{$p$-value} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\rotatebox{90}{\\scriptsize Budget}}\n& 512 vs.\\ 256           & $+$1.60  & [$-$0.34, $+$3.53]   & 0.117 \\\\\n& 1024 vs.\\ 512          & $-$3.19  & [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]   & 0.002** \\\\\n& no\\_limit vs.\\ 1024    & $+$4.54  & [$+$2.52, $+$6.55]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& no\\_limit vs.\\ 256     & $+$2.94  & [$+$0.84, $+$5.04]   & 0.006** \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\rotatebox{90}{\\scriptsize vs.\\ no\\_trace}}\n& 256 $-$ no\\_trace       & $+$3.70  & [$+$1.43, $+$6.05]   & 0.002** \\\\\n& 512 $-$ no\\_trace       & $+$5.29  & [$+$3.03, $+$7.56]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& 1024 $-$ no\\_trace      & $+$2.10  & [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]   & 0.078 \\\\\n& no\\_limit $-$ no\\_trace & $+$6.64  & [$+$4.37, $+$8.99]   & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{\\rotatebox{90}{\\scriptsize vs.\\ exc.}}\n& 256 $-$ exc\\_only       & $-$21.01 & [$-$23.45, $-$18.74] & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& 512 $-$ exc\\_only       & $-$19.41 & [$-$21.76, $-$17.14] & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& 1024 $-$ exc\\_only      & $-$22.61 & [$-$25.04, $-$20.25] & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n& no\\_limit $-$ exc\\_only & $-$18.07 & [$-$20.34, $-$15.88] & ${<}$0.001*** \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Non-monotonic pattern (head-truncation).}\nUnder head-truncation (retaining trace prefixes), the trend is non-monotonic: 512 tokens (80.42\\%) slightly outperforms 256 tokens (78.82\\%), but doubling to 1024 tokens \\emph{decreases} accuracy to 77.23\\%, a statistically significant 3.19pp drop ($p{=}.002$, 95\\% CI [$-$5.29, $-$1.09]).\nThe no-limit condition (81.76\\%) recovers and significantly exceeds 1024 tokens ($p{<}.001$), but 1024 tokens is not significantly better than \\texttt{no\\_trace} (75.13\\%; $\\Delta{=}{+}2.10$pp, $p{=}.078$, 95\\% CI [$-$0.17, $+$4.37]).\nThis non-monotonicity is specific to head-truncation: the 1024-token prefix retains enough irrelevant content to mislead the model while discarding diagnostic events that appear near the trace terminus.\nUnder tail-truncation, accuracy increases monotonically for Qwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}); only DeepSeek-6.7B shows a mild $-$1.5pp decrease at tail-1024$\\to$tail-2048, attributable to architecture-specific long-context sensitivity rather than a systematic mid-length pathology.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nAll four trace budget conditions significantly exceed \\texttt{no\\_trace} except 1024 tokens ($p{=}.078$), confirming that raw trace content provides some signal but unreliably so.\nThe gap to \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) is massive for every budget condition (18--23pp, all $p{<}.001$), underscoring that even unlimited raw traces cannot substitute for targeted signal extraction.\nThe best full-trace condition (no\\_limit, 81.76\\%) still lags \\texttt{exception\\_only} by 18.07pp (95\\% CI [$-$20.34, $-$15.88]).\n\n\\paragraph{Interpretation.}\nThese results rule out input truncation as the primary explanation for the full-trace performance gap: even with unlimited tokens, the model cannot extract the diagnostic signal that VOTA's targeted compression provides.\nThe non-monotonic dip at 1024 tokens is consistent with a ``worst-of-both-worlds'' regime where the model receives enough noisy context to dilute the signal but not enough to compensate through broader coverage.\nThe result reinforces the information density interpretation (\\S\\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \\emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \\emph{length}.\nIn contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\n\n\\paragraph{Truncation direction.}\nTail-truncation (retaining the last $k$ tokens) dramatically outperforms head-truncation: \\texttt{tail\\_2048} achieves 97.31\\% vs.\\ \\texttt{head\\_2048} (no\\_limit) at 81.76\\%, a 15.55pp improvement.\nEven \\texttt{tail\\_512} (93.61\\%) exceeds every head-truncation condition by over 11pp.\n\nHowever, \\texttt{tail\\_256} (77.48\\%) performs at the \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline level (75.13\\%), indicating that the diagnostic signal does not reside in the very last trace lines.\nThe critical jump occurs between 256 and 512 tail tokens (+16.13pp), localizing the signal window to approximately 256--512 tokens from the trace terminus.\nThis is consistent with the trace format: the globals module dump consumes the first ${\\sim}$384 tail tokens (pure noise), so structured execution markers (\\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values) only become visible when the budget exceeds this threshold.\n\nThe 18.07pp gap from \\texttt{full\\_trace} to \\texttt{exception\\_only} decomposes as: 86\\% positional concentration (recovered by tail-truncation at 2048 tokens) and 14\\% residual content noise (the 2.52pp gap from \\texttt{tail\\_2048} to \\texttt{exception\\_only} that persists regardless of truncation direction).\nThe fact that \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) still outperforms \\texttt{tail\\_2048} (97.31\\%) confirms that semantic extraction (identifying the exception type and message) is superior to positional extraction (keeping a fixed window), because the model need not filter out non-diagnostic lines that happen to fall within the window.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model budget curve.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model} extends the tail-truncation analysis to a full budget sweep across four models spanning three architecture families and an 8.7$\\times$ parameter range.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model tail-truncation budget curve ($N{=}1{,}190$). All four models exhibit a transition between tail-384 and tail-512 (+11--22pp). $\\downarrow$: non-monotonic decrease. $^\\dag$CodeLlama tail-384/512/1024 trained with label-only loss to address tokenizer-driven gradient dilution (\\S\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}); tail-1024 reports best-epoch accuracy (epoch 1). CodeLlama tail entries are not directly comparable to other models (which use full-sequence loss). $^\\ddag$CodeLlama tail-2048 outlier (41.8\\%): LoRA long-sequence instability under label-only loss; excluded from cross-model analysis.}\n\\label{tab:tail_cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{tail-256} & \\textbf{tail-384} & \\textbf{tail-512} & \\textbf{tail-1024} & \\textbf{tail-2048} & \\textbf{exc\\_only} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-7B (7B) & 78.9 & 79.9 & 93.6 & 96.5 & 97.3 & 99.83 \\\\\nDeepSeek (6.7B) & 76.4 & 80.7 & 93.9 & 96.5 & 95.0$\\downarrow$ & 99.33 \\\\\nCodeLlama (13B) & 77.9 & 82.7$^\\dag$ & 98.2$^\\dag$ & 98.2$^\\dag$ & 41.8$^\\ddag$ & 99.89 \\\\\nQwen-1.5B (1.5B) & 72.6 & 73.6 & 95.6 & 96.6 & 97.9 & 99.83 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model truncation direction comparison ($N{=}1{,}190$). Head-truncation retains the first 2,048 tokens; tail-truncation retains the last 2,048. All four models' head-2048 accuracies converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp), comparable to the tail-256 signal-poor band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp). $^\\dag$CodeLlama tail-2048 pending retraining (\\S\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:head_vs_tail}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{head-2048 (\\%)} & \\textbf{tail-2048 (\\%)} & \\textbf{Gap (pp)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B & 75.3 & 97.9 & +22.6 \\\\\nDeepSeek-6.7B & 78.7 & 95.0 & +16.3 \\\\\nCodeLlama-13B & 79.9 & ---$^\\dag$ & ---$^\\dag$ \\\\\nQwen-7B & 81.8 & 97.3 & +15.5 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nFour patterns emerge from the budget curve.\n\\emph{Phase transition between 384 and 512 tokens}: the tail-384 column reveals that the transition is not instantaneous at 256$\\to$512 but localized between 384 and 512 tokens.\nAt tail-384, all four models remain near baseline (73.6--80.7\\%), gaining only +1.0--4.8pp over tail-256.\nThe steep jump occurs at 384$\\to$512: +13.7pp (Qwen-7B), +13.2pp (DeepSeek), +15.5pp (CodeLlama), +22.0pp (Qwen-1.5B), crossing from near-baseline to near-ceiling.\nThis localizes the critical threshold to ${\\sim}$400--500 tokens: below ${\\sim}$384 tokens, the entire token budget is consumed by globals module dumps (pure noise); above ${\\sim}$512, at least one \\texttt{[Line~X]} execution event marker becomes visible, regardless of model capacity or architecture.\n\n\\emph{Convergence at tail-512+}: once the sharp performance jump is crossed, accuracy converges across all models into a 93.6--98.2\\% band at tail-512 (seed 42).\nMulti-seed validation confirms robustness: across all 12 model$\\times$seed combinations at tail-512 (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}), all accuracies fall within [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%], a 4.63pp band that compresses the signal-poor tail-256 spread (6.3pp) by 27\\%.\nCodeLlama-13B shows perfect per-model convergence at tail-512: all three seeds achieve 98.24\\% ($\\sigma{=}$0), consistent with the 13B scale and label-only loss providing a stronger optimization signal in the signal-rich regime (Table~\\ref{tab:tail512_seeds}).\nHowever, CodeLlama's higher tail-512 accuracy may partly reflect label-only loss rather than model capacity; direct comparison with other models (which use full-sequence loss) is confounded by this training difference.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-seed convergence at the tail-512 truncation point ($N{=}1{,}190$ per seed). All 12 model$\\times$seed combinations fall within a 4.63pp band [93.61\\%, 98.24\\%]. CodeLlama-13B achieves identical accuracy across all three seeds ($\\sigma{=}$0).}\n\\label{tab:tail512_seeds}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{S42} & \\textbf{S123} & \\textbf{S456} & \\textbf{Mean} & \\textbf{$\\sigma$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-7B (7B)      & 93.61 & 96.00 & 97.65 & 95.75 & 2.05 \\\\\nDeepSeek (6.7B)    & 93.95 & 95.04 & 96.10 & 95.03 & 1.08 \\\\\nQwen-1.5B (1.5B)  & 95.63 & 97.65 & 96.72 & 96.67 & 1.01 \\\\\nCodeLlama (13B)    & 98.24 & 98.24 & 98.24 & \\textbf{98.24} & \\textbf{0.00} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{Cross-model band} & \\multicolumn{2}{r}{[93.61, 98.24] = 4.63pp} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt tail-1024, four models reach 96.5--98.2\\% (CodeLlama 98.2\\% under label-only loss, three others 96.5--96.6\\%).\nScale and architecture differences that dominate the signal-poor regime (tail-256 range: 72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp) compress dramatically in the signal-rich regime.\n\n\\emph{Signal-poor convergence band}: head-2048 accuracies across all four models also converge into a narrow 75.3--81.8\\% band (6.5pp; Table~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail}), comparable in width to the tail-256 band (72.6--78.9\\%, 6.3pp).\nWhen the diagnostic signal is absent (head-truncation discards trace terminus) or unreachable (tail-256 is too short), models of different scale and architecture collapse to similar performance, suggesting a shared floor determined by code comprehension rather than trace processing.\n\n\\emph{Scale behavior inversion}: in the signal-poor regime (tail-256), smaller models perform worse (Qwen-1.5B 72.6\\% vs.\\ Qwen-7B 78.9\\%, $-$6.3pp), mirroring the \\texttt{no\\_trace} scale pattern.\nIn the signal-rich regime (tail-512+), this reverses: Qwen-1.5B matches or exceeds Qwen-7B (95.6\\% vs.\\ 93.6\\% at tail-512; 97.9\\% vs.\\ 97.3\\% at tail-2048), confirming that sufficient trace signal eliminates the capacity advantage.\n\n\\emph{Non-monotonicity at long sequences}: two models show accuracy decreases beyond tail-1024.\nDeepSeek-6.7B drops $-$1.5pp from tail-1024 (96.5\\%) to tail-2048 (95.0\\%), consistent with its known long-context sensitivity (\\S\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\nCodeLlama-13B exhibits a more severe collapse: tail-2048 under label-only loss drops to 41.8\\% (vs.\\ tail-1024 = 98.2\\%), which we attribute to LoRA long-sequence instability under the label-only loss regime; this outlier is excluded from cross-model tail-2048 analysis.\nQwen-7B and Qwen-1.5B increase monotonically across all budgets.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:head_vs_tail} confirms the truncation direction effect: among the three models with complete data, tail-2048 outperforms head-2048 by 15.5--22.6pp.\nThe gap is largest for Qwen-1.5B (+22.6pp), indicating that smaller models benefit most from positional proximity to the diagnostic signal, as they lack the capacity to locate it within longer, noisier prefixes.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Calibration Analysis}\n\\label{sec:calibration_full}\n\nBeyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \\citep{spiess2025calibration}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Calibration metrics on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score. Lower is better for both.}\n\\label{tab:calibration}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nae\\_only & \\textbf{0.0004} & \\textbf{0.0008} \\\\\nexception\\_only & 0.0027 & 0.0013 \\\\\nVOTA & 0.0054 & 0.005 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 0.096 & 0.144 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 0.171 & 0.197 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.\nTier~1 conditions (\\texttt{ae\\_only}: ECE=0.0004; \\texttt{exception\\_only}: ECE=0.0027) produce near-perfectly calibrated predictions, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} (ECE=0.096) and \\texttt{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibit substantial miscalibration.\nVOTA (ECE=0.0054) is well-calibrated but slightly worse than either Tier~1 component alone, reinforcing the pattern that combining trace signals dilutes rather than improves the prediction signal.\n\nThe Brier scores tell a consistent story: \\texttt{ae\\_only} (0.0008) and \\texttt{exception\\_only} (0.0013) achieve near-zero scores, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} (0.197) indicates substantial miscalibration.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model calibration.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:calibration_cross} extends the calibration analysis to four models (1.5B--13B) under \\texttt{exception\\_only}.\nAll four models achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007 and Brier ${<}$ 0.007, confirming that calibration quality generalizes across architectures and scales.\nCodeLlama-13B achieves the lowest ECE (0.0020) and MCE (0.0411), while DeepSeek-6.7B shows the highest ECE (0.0064) and Brier (0.0061), consistent with its higher error rate (8 errors vs.\\ 2 for Qwen/CodeLlama).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-model calibration for \\texttt{exception\\_only} ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). ECE = expected calibration error; Brier = Brier score; MCE = maximum calibration error. All four models (1.5B--13B) achieve ECE ${<}$ 0.007.}\n\\label{tab:calibration_cross}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{MCE $\\downarrow$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B & \\textbf{0.0020} & \\textbf{0.0013} & --- \\\\\nCodeLlama-13B & \\textbf{0.0020} & \\textbf{0.0013} & \\textbf{0.0411} \\\\\nQwen-7B & 0.0027 & \\textbf{0.0013} & 0.8355 \\\\\nDeepSeek-6.7B & 0.0064 & 0.0061 & 0.7756 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_calibration}\n\\caption{Reliability diagrams for four trace conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B ($N{=}1{,}190$, seed=42). \\texttt{exception\\_only} (ECE=0.0027) clusters near the diagonal, while \\texttt{no\\_trace} (ECE=0.171) exhibits systematic overconfidence, a 63$\\times$ gap.}\n\\label{fig:calibration}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Error Overlap Analysis}\n\\label{sec:error_overlap}\n\nWe analyze the overlap between error sets of different conditions to determine whether they fail on the same samples (Figure~\\ref{fig:error_overlap}).\n\n\\texttt{exception\\_only}'s 2 errors (samples 901 and 905) have zero intersection with the error sets of all other conditions (Jaccard = 0.000 in all pairwise comparisons).\nThis orthogonality arises because \\texttt{exception\\_only} errors are caused by \\texttt{GeneratorExit} false positives (pass samples misclassified as fail due to benign \\texttt{BaseException} events), while errors from other conditions are predominantly fail samples misclassified as pass due to insufficient signal.\n\nAmong non-exception conditions, error sets exhibit hierarchical containment: \\texttt{ae\\_only} $\\subset$ VOTA $\\subset$ \\texttt{loop\\_only} $\\subset$ \\texttt{no\\_trace}, with pairwise Jaccard indices increasing with signal overlap.\n\\texttt{loop\\_only} and \\texttt{full\\_trace} share the highest overlap (Jaccard ${\\sim}$0.65), reflecting their shared reliance on behavioral patterns rather than explicit fault indicators.\n\nThis analysis confirms that \\texttt{exception\\_only} operates on an independent signal dimension: its errors are representation ambiguities (iterator protocol artifacts) rather than information deficits, explaining why combining it with other signals cannot eliminate these specific errors.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{error_overlap}\n\\caption{Jaccard similarity between error sets of different conditions (Qwen-7B, seed=42, $N{=}1{,}190$). \\texttt{exception\\_only} (exc\\_only) has zero overlap with all other conditions, confirming it operates on an independent signal dimension.}\n\\label{fig:error_overlap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Cross-Dataset Generalization: DebugBench}\n\\label{sec:debugbench_full}\n\nTo test whether exception-based verdict prediction generalizes beyond LLM-generated solutions, we evaluate on DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench}, a dataset of human-written programs with synthetically injected bugs (single-point mutations such as operator replacement, variable misuse, and missing statements).\nAfter filtering for executable Python samples with valid traces, we retain 426 samples with a near-balanced class distribution (majority baseline: 51.4\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is preserved, and exception\\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}\n\\label{tab:debugbench}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{100.00} & [99.14, 100.00] \\\\\nVOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\\\\nno\\_trace & 96.71 & [94.80, 98.40] \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 51.41 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (100.00\\%) $\\geq$ VOTA (99.77\\%) $>$ \\texttt{no\\_trace} (96.71\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition achieves perfect accuracy, confirming that the exception-based representation generalizes to a different fault distribution without degradation.\n\nThe absolute margins are much narrower than on the main dataset: the \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline already achieves 96.71\\% (compared to 75.13\\% on HumanEval+MBPP), leaving only 3.29pp of headroom for trace-based improvement.\nThis ceiling effect reflects a fundamental difference in fault characteristics: DebugBench bugs are synthetically injected single-point mutations, which produce code that is structurally dissimilar from the original correct solution; a model can often identify the fault from source code alone.\nIn contrast, the HumanEval+MBPP solutions are LLM-generated, producing natural errors (logic mistakes, edge-case failures) where the faulty and correct solutions may be structurally similar, making code-only verdict prediction substantially harder.\n\nThese results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach: the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is consistent across both datasets. However, the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.\nThe 24.70pp trace lift on the main dataset (no\\_trace 75.13\\% $\\to$ exception\\_only 99.83\\%) remains the primary evidence for the value of exception-based trace signals.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Cross-Model Robustness Analysis}\n\\label{sec:robustness_analysis}\n\nThe cross-model gap pattern (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \\emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}\nException events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity (a fixed-format error message) ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.\nAll three DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} seeds achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% (99.33/99.50/99.33, $\\sigma{=}$0.10pp), matching Qwen and CodeLlama at ${\\geq}$99.83\\%.\nVOTA achieves ${\\geq}$98.57\\% across all models when excluding outlier seeds (cross-model range: 0.80pp), but DeepSeek VOTA exhibits seed-dependent instability: seed 42 (84.45\\%) is a training failure due to tokenizer-induced truncation (91.7\\% of training samples affected), while seeds 123/456 (99.16\\%/99.33\\%) match cross-model performance.\nThis VOTA instability is confounded by tokenizer efficiency and base model differences \\citep{yang2024generalization}.\nOur \\texttt{exception\\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely: inputs have a median of 150 tokens (mean 398), well below the 2{,}048-token context window, so no training samples are truncated on either model.\nThe resulting 0.50pp cross-model gap (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.\nThe practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}\n\\label{sec:training_dynamics}\n\nThe DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} results are consistent across all three seeds (99.33/99.50/99.33, $\\sigma{=}$0.10pp), matching Qwen's cross-seed stability.\nEpoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in seed 42: accuracy peaks at 99.83\\% after epoch~1 but settles at 99.33\\% by epoch~3 ($-$0.50pp), while seed 456 shows no degradation (99.33\\% at both epochs).\nEarly stopping may yield marginal gains, but the epoch effect is small (${\\leq}$0.5pp).\nThe practical recommendation: for deployment, use task-specific metrics (accuracy, fail recall) for checkpoint and seed selection rather than training loss, and train multiple seeds to select the best.\n\nThe contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA seed 42 accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\\% to 84.45\\% across epochs, consistent with a performance limit from high trace truncation (91.7\\% of samples; \\S\\ref{sec:tokenizer_analysis}).\nSeeds 123/456, which avoid the truncation-sensitive initialization basin, achieve 99.16\\%/99.33\\%, confirming that the deficit is seed-specific rather than a fundamental model limitation.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Deduplication Statistics}\n\\label{sec:dedup_stats}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dedup} reports the duplication rates across trace formats.\nBecause multiple passing solutions to the same problem produce identical (input, output) pairs in the \\texttt{exception\\_only} format (correct executions raise no exceptions, so the trace content is the same regardless of the solution's internal logic), the dataset contains duplicate rows.\nTrain--test input overlap is zero (split by problem ID), so no information leakage occurs.\nThe high duplication in \\texttt{exception\\_only} does not affect our experimental conclusions: after deduplication the training set still contains 2,858 unique samples and the test set 607, providing sufficient coverage for the 591-problem benchmark.\n\n\\paragraph{Problem-level partitioning and sensitivity analysis.}\nData is partitioned at the problem level: 472 training problems and 119 test problems, with zero crossover.\nDuplicates arise from multiple candidate solutions per problem (up to 10 solutions each), not from cross-problem overlap.\nTo verify that deduplication does not alter any finding, we ran a sensitivity analysis across all 19 experimental conditions (7 trace formats $\\times$ 4 models, minus conditions not run): deduplication produces a 0.00pp accuracy delta in every case.\nThis confirms that duplicate samples neither inflate nor deflate accuracy, because duplicates share the same ground-truth label and the model's prediction is deterministic at inference time.\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set contains 48.99\\% duplicate inputs due to representation compactness; all confidence intervals treat samples as independent Bernoulli trials.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Deduplication statistics. The \\texttt{exception\\_only} format collapses multiple passing solutions into identical rows because correct executions produce no exception events. Train--test input overlap is 0 (no leakage).}\n\\label{tab:dedup}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Split} & \\textbf{Total} & \\textbf{Unique} & \\textbf{Dup.\\ rate} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only train & 4{,}720 & 2{,}858 & 39.45\\% \\\\\nexception\\_only test  & 1{,}190 &   607 & 48.99\\% \\\\\nraw\\_traces (full)    & 5{,}910 & 5{,}803 &  1.81\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Regex Baseline Analysis}\n\\label{sec:regex_baselines}\n\nTo establish a non-learning upper bound, we evaluate six regex-based baselines on the \\texttt{exception\\_only} test set ($N{=}1{,}190$) and two format-specific baselines on VOTA and \\texttt{full\\_trace} inputs.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Regex baseline accuracy ($N{=}1{,}190$). B4 is the strongest semantic baseline (6.22pp below SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only}, McNemar $p{<}.001$). B6 uses an error count threshold. $^\\ddag$B5 exploits a data formatting artifact: the \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} marker is deterministically present in all pass samples, making this a format-level shortcut rather than a genuine exception-based heuristic.}\n\\label{tab:regex}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Baseline} & \\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nB1: \\texttt{[ERROR]} presence & string match & 93.45 \\\\\nB2: Traceback presence & string match & 71.51 \\\\\nB3: Any keyword & union of B1+B2+Exception & 93.45 \\\\\nB4: Exception type regex & regex on exception class names & \\textbf{93.61} \\\\\nB5: Format marker & \\texttt{[No exception data]} $\\to$ pass & 99.33$^\\ddag$ \\\\\nB6: Error count $\\geq$2 & count threshold & 92.02 \\\\\n\\midrule\nVOTA format regex & best regex on VOTA traces & 75.55 \\\\\nfull\\_trace format regex & best regex on full traces & 77.39 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe strongest legitimate baseline (B4, 93.61\\%) applies regex pattern-matching on Python exception class names (e.g., \\texttt{ValueError}, \\texttt{TypeError}).\nIts errors concentrate on 8 boundary samples where a \\texttt{ValueError} is raised inside a \\texttt{try/except} block and caught; the program passes despite an exception occurring in the trace.\nSFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} correctly classifies 6 of these 8 samples, indicating that the model learns the semantic distinction between caught (benign) and uncaught (fault-indicating) exceptions.\nB5 (99.33\\%) achieves near-SFT accuracy but relies on the deterministic presence of a format marker in pass-class traces, which is an artifact of the trace preprocessing pipeline rather than a generalizable signal; McNemar test vs.\\ SFT yields $p{=}.041$, confirming a statistically significant difference.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-format comparison.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:regex_cross_format} compares SFT and the best applicable regex baseline for each trace format.\nThe SFT advantage increases with format complexity: \\texttt{no\\_trace} (+3.62pp over majority baseline) $<$ \\texttt{full\\_trace} (+4.37pp) $<$ \\texttt{exception\\_only} (+6.22pp) $\\ll$ VOTA (+23.86pp).\nThe VOTA gap is largest because its structured multi-component format carries semantic content that regex cannot parse, while \\texttt{exception\\_only}'s simpler format allows regex to capture most of the signal.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{SFT vs.\\ best regex baseline by trace format ($N{=}1{,}190$). Regex baselines: majority class for \\texttt{no\\_trace}, keyword matching for \\texttt{full\\_trace}, pattern matching for VOTA, exception type regex (B4) for \\texttt{exception\\_only}.}\n\\label{tab:regex_cross_format}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Format} & \\textbf{SFT (\\%)} & \\textbf{Regex (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & 99.83 & 93.61 & +6.22 \\\\\nVOTA & 99.05 & 75.55 & +23.50 \\\\\nfull\\_trace & 81.76 & 77.39 & +4.37 \\\\\nno\\_trace & 75.13 & 71.51 & +3.62 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Cross-Model Zero-Shot Comparison}\n\\label{sec:zeroshot_cross_model}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:zeroshot_cross_model} compares zero-shot and SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} accuracy across four models.\nZero-shot performance does not correlate with model size: the smallest model (Qwen-1.5B, 93.45\\%) outperforms all larger models, while the largest (CodeLlama-13B) falls below majority baseline.\nSFT compresses the 42.44pp zero-shot range to 0.50pp, eliminating architecture-dependent variance.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Zero-shot vs.\\ SFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} across four models ($N{=}1{,}190$). Zero-shot accuracy is uncorrelated with model size; SFT normalizes cross-architecture variance from 42.44pp to 0.50pp.}\n\\label{tab:zeroshot_cross_model}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Size} & \\textbf{Zero-shot (\\%)} & \\textbf{SFT (\\%)} & \\textbf{$\\Delta$ (pp)} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-1.5B & 1.5B & 93.45 & 99.83 & +6.38 \\\\\nDeepSeek-6.7B & 6.7B & 90.59 & 99.39 & +8.80 \\\\\nQwen-7B & 7B & 89.08 & 99.83 & +10.75 \\\\\nCodeLlama-13B & 13B & 51.01 & 99.89 & +48.88 \\\\\n\\midrule\nRange & --- & 42.44pp & 0.50pp & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Analysis}\n\\label{sec:calibration_analysis}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Model calibration on \\texttt{exception\\_only} (seed 42). All four models achieve ECE $<$ 0.005, confirming that near-perfect accuracy reflects genuine confidence rather than overfit predictions.}\n\\label{tab:calibration_model_level}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Accuracy} & \\textbf{ECE $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Brier $\\downarrow$} & \\textbf{Mean Conf.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen-2.5-1.5B & 99.83\\% & 0.0014 & 0.0013 & 99.82\\% \\\\\nQwen-2.5-7B & 99.83\\% & 0.0017 & 0.0013 & 99.80\\% \\\\\nCodeLlama-13B & 99.83\\% & 0.0020 & 0.0013 & 99.63\\% \\\\\nDeepSeek-6.7B & 99.33\\% & 0.0048 & 0.0059 & 99.71\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n",

_source line 8195 · 2026-05-22T13:21:05.717Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HMNmdgLoXLDqbQYfJQb6qZ`</summary>

```text
99	
100	Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%$\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\%$\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.
101	Removing assertion points has no effect (\texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae} = 99.41\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.
102	The Tier~1/2 boundary is unambiguous: the lowest Tier~1 condition (\texttt{ae\_only} on DeepSeek, 98.53\%$\pm$0.30, 2 seeds) exceeds the highest Tier~2 (\texttt{loop\_only} on Qwen-7B, 86.05\%, CI [83.97, 87.96]) by 12.48\,pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.
103	Gap statistic analysis independently supports $k{=}2$ clusters for 3/4 models and pooled data (silhouette ${\geq}$0.74).
104	Tier~2 \texttt{loop\_only} lifts over \texttt{no\_trace} are model-dependent: Qwen shows +10.92pp (7B) and +7.19pp (1.5B, $p{<}.0001$), with the larger 7B gain likely reflecting greater capacity to exploit structured loop patterns; DeepSeek +6.95pp (80.31\%), CodeLlama +0.93pp (77.40\%, n.s.).
```

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/appendix.tex",
  "old_string": "The \\texttt{GeneratorExit} case represents a semantic ambiguity in the trace format rather than a model failure, as discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:irreducible}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Trace Length Ablation}",
  "new_string": "The \\texttt{GeneratorExit} case represents a semantic ambiguity in the trace format rather than a model failure, as discussed in \\S\\ref{sec:irreducible}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Exception Subcomponent Ablation}\n\\label{sec:exc_subcomponent}\n\nTo test whether verdict prediction depends on a specific exception subcomponent or merely the binary presence of an exception event, we train Qwen-2.5-7B on three subcomponent-only conditions (seed 42, $N{=}1{,}190$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Exception subcomponent ablation (Qwen-2.5-7B, seed 42, $N{=}1{,}190$). All three subcomponents independently achieve ${\\geq}$99.50\\%. $^\\dag$Retains \\texttt{[ERROR]} format marker; $^\\ddag$no format markers, bare exception class name only.}\n\\label{tab:exc_subcomponent}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{Fail R} & \\textbf{Pass R} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexc\\_type\\_only$^\\dag$ & \\textbf{100.00} & 100.00 & 100.00 \\\\\nexc\\_stacktrace\\_only$^\\dag$ & 99.83 & 100.00 & 99.76 \\\\\nexc\\_message\\_only$^\\ddag$ & 99.50 & 100.00 & 99.29 \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only (ref.) & 99.83 & 100.00 & 99.76 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll three subcomponents independently achieve ${\\geq}$99.50\\% with 100\\% fail recall (Table~\\ref{tab:exc_subcomponent}), confirming that no single subcomponent is necessary for Tier~1 performance.\n\n\\paragraph{Format marker confound.}\n\\texttt{exc\\_type\\_only} and \\texttt{exc\\_stacktrace\\_only} retain the \\texttt{[ERROR]} prefix marker present in the full \\texttt{exception\\_only} format; \\texttt{exc\\_message\\_only} contains only the bare exception class name and message text without any format markers.\nBecause the marker alone is sufficient for near-perfect regex accuracy (B5: 99.33\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}), \\texttt{exc\\_type\\_only} (100.00\\%) and \\texttt{exc\\_stacktrace\\_only} (99.83\\%) are confounded with marker-based classification; we therefore do not make hierarchy claims among subcomponents.\n\\texttt{exc\\_message\\_only} (99.50\\%) provides a clean test: without any format markers, it achieves accuracy comparable to the full \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition (99.83\\%), complementing the dedicated marker-removal ablation (\\texttt{nomarker}: 97.65\\%; \\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}) as additional evidence that the model learns semantic exception patterns rather than format shortcuts.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\section{Trace Length Ablation}"
}

### Tool result

_source line 8223 · 2026-05-22T13:21:50.509Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01L2thKBtwqZKzPiFMqhB2Ar`</summary>

```text
100	Tier~1 signals are individually sufficient: \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%$\pm$0.00) exceeds VOTA (99.05\%$\pm$0.38) by +0.78pp (2/3 seeds $p{<}.05$, all directionally consistent), implying that combining signals introduces noise.
101	Removing assertion points has no effect (\texttt{VOTA\_no\_ae} = 99.41\%, single seed), confirming exception dominance.
102	Individual exception subcomponents (type, stacktrace, message) each independently achieve ${\geq}$99.50\%, confirming that the binary presence of an exception event, rather than any specific subcomponent, drives Tier~1 performance (Appendix~\ref{sec:exc_subcomponent}).
103	The Tier~1/2 boundary is unambiguous: the lowest Tier~1 condition (\texttt{ae\_only} on DeepSeek, 98.53\%$\pm$0.30, 2 seeds) exceeds the highest Tier~2 (\texttt{loop\_only} on Qwen-7B, 86.05\%, CI [83.97, 87.96]) by 12.48\,pp with non-overlapping confidence intervals.
104	Gap statistic analysis independently supports $k{=}2$ clusters for 3/4 models and pooled data (silhouette ${\geq}$0.74).
105	Tier~2 \texttt{loop\_only} lifts over \texttt{no\_trace} are model-dependent: Qwen shows +10.92pp (7B) and +7.19pp (1.5B, $p{<}.0001$), with the larger 7B gain likely reflecting greater capacity to exploit structured loop patterns; DeepSeek +6.95pp (80.31\%), CodeLlama +0.93pp (77.40\%, n.s.).
106	\paragraph{Rule-based baseline comparison.}
107	The strongest non-trivial regex heuristic (B4: exception class name matching; Table~\ref{tab:regex}) achieves 93.61\% accuracy (F1=0.876), trailing SFT \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%, F1=0.997) by 6.22pp (McNemar $p{<}.001$).
```

### Tool result

_source line 8240 · 2026-05-22T13:22:22.332Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KabneyPDVMXNZQbrwEu6R4`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}
5	The results suggest a descriptive two-pathway framework---not a causal model---for why complete traces degrade SFT learning.
6	
7	\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}
8	The diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\S2.1) correlates with accuracy at $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$) across five conditions, and all four models achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% under \texttt{exception\_only} with zero variance on Qwen ($\sigma{=}$0.00, 3 seeds).
9	Although 55.5\% of failures are silent logic errors, \texttt{exception\_only} achieves 100\% fail recall because test assertions generate \texttt{AssertionError} events.
10	\texttt{ae\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms \texttt{exception\_only} (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that additional content introduces noise.
11	
12	\paragraph{Implicit pathway: terminal execution state.}
13	Tail-truncated traces achieve 93--97\% accuracy (Table~\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}) despite $d {\approx} 0$.
14	Tail truncation at ${\geq}$512 tokens captures \texttt{[Line~X]} variable values at final execution steps, which indirectly encode program outcome; below 384 tokens, the globals module dump consumes the entire budget, yielding ${\sim}$73\% ($\approx$ \texttt{no\_trace}).
15	
16	\paragraph{Diagnostic signal dilution in complete traces.}
17	Both pathways' signals exist in full traces but are buried under thousands of intermediate tokens.
18	Controlled gradient masking confirms this: $-$0.84pp under 99.8\% mask limits gradient dilution to ${\leq}$5\% of the gap (\S\ref{sec:density}).
19	Figure~\ref{fig:training_dynamics} confirms: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps (std\,=\,0 by step 200), while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates (82--89\%) without convergence (final std\,=\,0.014); eval accuracy CV differs 9.4$\times$ (0.022 vs.\ 0.002), with a maximum single-step drop of 5.29pp (Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
20	
21	\begin{figure}[t]
22	\centering
23	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}
24	\caption{Training dynamics: \texttt{exception\_only} converges to 99.83\% by step 150; \texttt{full\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates (82--89\%) without convergence.}
25	\label{fig:training_dynamics}
26	\end{figure}
27	
28	\paragraph{Why SFT over regex?}
29	SFT \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) exceeds the best regex (B5, 99.33\%) by 0.50pp on caught-exception boundary cases (McNemar $p{=}.014$), provides calibrated probabilities (ECE\,=\,0.0027; \S\ref{sec:calibration}), and retains 97.65\% under marker removal vs.\ B5's 28.49\% (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
30	Bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) with near-majority \texttt{no\_trace} performance confirms the learned patterns are language-level, not dataset-specific.
31	
32	\paragraph{The marginal value of complete traces.}
33	Source code alone achieves 73--76\% (\texttt{no\_trace}), so traces add at most ${\sim}$27pp; complete traces capture only 4--9pp of this headroom while exception events capture 24--27pp.
34	That more information degrades performance aligns with feature selection theory \citep{guyon2003introduction}, the Information Bottleneck \citep{tishby2000information}, and LIMA \citep{zhou2023lima}.
35	
36	\paragraph{Design implications.}
37	The two-tier hierarchy suggests a practical fallback: extract exception events first; resort to tail-truncated state otherwise.
38	Tier~2 benefit is model-dependent (Qwen +10.9pp; CodeLlama +0.9pp), so trace design should match deployment model capacity.
39	
40	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}\nThe results suggest a descriptive two-pathway framework---not a causal model---for why complete traces degrade SFT learning.\n\n\\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}\nThe diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\\S2.1) correlates with accuracy at $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$) across five conditions, and all four models achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% under \\texttt{exception\\_only} with zero variance on Qwen ($\\sigma{=}$0.00, 3 seeds).\nAlthough 55.5\\% of failures are silent logic errors, \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 100\\% fail recall because test assertions generate \\texttt{AssertionError} events.\n\\texttt{ae\\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that additional content introduces noise.\n\n\\paragraph{Implicit pathway: terminal execution state.}\nTail-truncated traces achieve 93--97\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}) despite $d {\\approx} 0$.\nTail truncation at ${\\geq}$512 tokens captures \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values at final execution steps, which indirectly encode program outcome; <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017DyhyUPdvQMnfv39rZnzQH`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Execution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.
4	The intuition is straightforward: more execution information should help the model make better predictions.
5	Yet for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), this intuition is wrong.
6	Complete traces \emph{actively degrade} accuracy compared to compact exception events, despite containing strictly more information.
7	
8	Prior work found trace utility to be task-dependent \citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks where output noise confounds the analysis.
9	Verdict prediction offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, and controlled component ablation directly measures how trace content affects SFT learning.
10	This approach aligns with recent *CL precedent: CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval} uses execution prediction to probe code reasoning, and NExT \citep{ni2024next} uses trace-augmented chain-of-thought for program repair.
11	
12	We present a systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.
13	Using 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program}, we evaluate four models across three architecture families and 1.5B to 13B parameters: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \citep{qwen2024coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \citep{deepseek2024coder}.
14	
15	The central result: complete traces (77--82\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\sim}$73--76\%) while exception events achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% across all models and seeds (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
16	A simple regex baseline (B5) that exploits the \texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker matches this ceiling at 99.33\%; even a trivial pattern suffices, so the interesting question is not why exceptions help but \emph{why complete traces hurt}.
17	Marker removal collapses B5 to 28.49\% while SFT retains 97.65\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) confirms that SFT acquires language-level diagnostic patterns beyond format matching (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
18	
19	A controlled gradient masking ablation limits gradient dilution to ${\leq}$5\% of the degradation; content quality---diagnostic signal density at inference, the mechanism underlying what is intuitively termed ``information overload''---accounts for ${\geq}$95\% (\S\ref{sec:density}).
20	Training dynamics confirm this: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps, while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates between 82--89\% without stable convergence (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
21	A tail-truncation budget sweep reveals a sharp performance jump at ${\sim}$512 tokens; this threshold coincides with the globals module dump (${\sim}$384 tokens), beyond which structured \texttt{[Line~X]} variable values appear (\S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
22	
23	Our contributions:
24	\begin{enumerate}
25	\item \textbf{Complete traces are strictly dominated by compact exception events}: across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete traces (77--82\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\sim}$73--76\%) while exception events achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}). Training dynamics directly show the mechanism: \texttt{exception\_only} converges within 50 steps while \texttt{full\_trace} oscillates without stable convergence (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).
26	
27	\item \textbf{SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching}: a regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\% while SFT retains 97.65\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\%) confirms the learned signal generalizes (\S\ref{sec:nomarker}).
28	
29	\item \textbf{A two-tier signal hierarchy with a practical trace selection strategy}: Tier~1 (exceptions, assertions; ${\geq}$98.53\%) dominates Tier~2 (loop compression; 77--86\%) by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs across all four models. A sharp performance transition at ${\sim}$512 tail tokens yields a practical fallback: exception events first, tail-truncated state otherwise (\S\ref{sec:ablation}, \S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
30	
31	\item \textbf{Controlled decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: gradient masking on \texttt{exception\_only} ($-$0.84pp under 99.8\% mask) limits gradient dilution to ${\leq}$5\% of the 18pp gap; content quality (diagnostic signal density at inference) is the dominant factor at ${\geq}$95\%. A complementary label-only ablation on the Qwen family yields a directionally consistent but confounded upper bound (${\leq}$26\%) (\S\ref{sec:density}).
32	\end{enumerate}
33	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Execution Traces for Code LLMs.}
4	TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples; \citet{wang2025dcsh} found trace utility task- and format-dependent, which we extend by showing that complete traces degrade SFT performance while compact exception events suffice.
5	NExT \citep{ni2024next} and ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} used trace chain-of-thought for repair and execution reasoning; \citet{ferrando2025execute} found compact traces comparable to full traces, supporting our density finding.
6	\citet{liu2023codeexecution} pre-trained on execution traces; CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval,xu2025cruxevalx} established execution reasoning benchmarks.
7	None decompose which trace components contribute to \emph{classification} accuracy.
8	
9	\paragraph{Code Verdict and Patch Classification.}
10	TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection; CC2Vec \citep{hoang2020cc2vec}, LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch}, and APPT \citep{appt2024} developed representation learning of code changes.
11	Verdict prediction is an instance of the test oracle problem \citep{barr2015oracle}; none isolate individual trace components.
12	
13	\paragraph{Fault Localization and Trace Selection.}
14	SBFL \citep{abreu2007accuracy,jones2002visualization,wong2014dstar} and LLM-based FL \citep{kang2024autofl,qin2024agentfl} share our insight that anomalous execution events carry the strongest diagnostic signal.
15	TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} and InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} use selective trace extraction; RepairAgent \citep{bouzenia2025repairagent}, Agentless \citep{xia2024agentless}, SWE-agent \citep{yang2024sweagent}, and OpenHands \citep{wang2025openhands} incorporate execution feedback in repair workflows \citep{jimenez2024swebench}, yet which trace components carry verdict signals most efficiently remains unstudied.
16	Prompt compression \citep{jiang2023llmlingua,li2023selectivecontext} showed pruning low-information tokens preserves accuracy; our decomposition quantifies \emph{why}.
17	
18	\paragraph{Calibration, Data Selection, and Defect Prediction.}
19	Neural network calibration \citep{guo2017calibration}, extended to LLMs \citep{kadavath2022language,geng2024calibration,spiess2025calibration}, studies confidence-correctness alignment; our ECE ${<}$ 0.005 suggests compact inputs improve calibration.
20	The Information Bottleneck \citep{tishby2000information}, feature selection theory \citep{guyon2003introduction}, and LIMA \citep{zhou2023lima} predict that compressing task-irrelevant information improves learning; data pruning \citep{paul2021deep,sorscher2022beyond} and curriculum learning \citep{bengio2009curriculum} confirm this empirically, consistent with our finding that compact exceptions converge rapidly while verbose traces oscillate.
21	Just-in-time defect prediction \citep{kamei2013large} shares our fault detection motivation.
22	
```

</details>

_source line 8281 · 2026-05-22T14:08:10.461Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GbkhPstQ8DW4fTiqssgioD`</summary>

```text
1	---
2	name: paper-writing-norms
3	description: 顶会级别论文写作风格规范——章节写法（Abstract/Intro/Related Work）、去 AI 风格、视觉密度、叙事哲学。Paper Agent 和 Worker 写论文章节时必须加载。
4	allowed-tools: [Read, Write, Edit, Glob, Grep]
5	scope: global
6	version: 2
7	priority: 9
8	tags:
9	  - writing
10	  - paper
11	  - style
12	  - norms
13	roles:
14	  - agent
15	  - worker
16	  - paper_agent
17	---
18	
19	# 论文写作风格规范
20	
21	> 核心哲学：**论文是一个有说服力的故事，不是实验报告。** 审稿人在几十秒内判断论文价值——如果 Abstract 和 Introduction 读起来像实验日志，论文就已经失败了。
22	
23	---
24	
25	## 一、总体写作哲学
26	
27	### 1.1 故事驱动，不是数据堆砌
28	
29	- 论文必须以"故事线"为核心展开：问题 → 洞察 → 方法 → 验证
30	- 禁止大量实验数据堆砌——每个数字出现必须服务于叙事推进
31	- 附录内容必须在正文中有明确引用且融入叙事，禁止简单堆砌
32	- 如果附录数据对核心叙事有重大影响，根据附录数据调整论文 framing
33	
34	### 1.2 主线统一性
35	
36	- 当 idea 在研究过程中发生变化后，**必须重审全文逻辑**
37	- 常见问题：Introduction 讲的故事 A，Method 做的是故事 B，Results 又在讲故事 C
38	- 修复方式：从最终实验结论反推，重写 Introduction 的问题定义和动机
39	- 每次 idea 调整后检查：Abstract → Introduction → Method → Results → Discussion → Conclusion 是否形成一条连贯主线
40	
41	### 1.3 专有名词必须有定义
42	
43	- 首次出现的专有名词/缩写必须给出完整定义和解释
44	- Abstract 中使用的缩写也必须在 Abstract 内定义（如 LLM = Large Language Model）
45	- 不能假设读者知道你自己定义的术语——论文从来没解释过的概念不能直接使用
46	- 通用规则：如果一个非标准术语第一次出现时没有括号定义，就是写作 bug
47	
48	### 1.4 写作前必读同类优秀论文
49	
50	- 每个章节动笔前，找 **2-3 篇目标会议近 1-2 年的高分论文**作为风格参考——同类问题或同类方法皆可
51	- 重点学：
52	  - Abstract 怎么用最少篇幅讲完故事
53	  - Introduction 怎么从大问题切入到具体贡献
54	  - Related Work 怎么 positioning 自己（不是 survey）
55	  - Table/Figure 怎么压缩信息密度
56	- **不是抄袭**，是学习同行的故事节奏和审美——不同子领域叙事节奏差异很大
57	- 实操：Paper Agent 可以从 `literature_map.yaml` 中挑 `role=foundation` 或 `role=direct-competitor` 的论文，把 `{title, venue, year, abstract}` 附在 Worker 的 task_prompt 中作为概念参考；如需学习写作风格本身，让 Worker 通过 WebSearch 取得论文链接并 fetch 全文
58	
59	---
60	
61	## 二、Abstract 写作规范
62	
63	### 2.1 四个必答问题（150-250 words）
64	
65	Abstract 必须在很短的篇幅内清楚回答：
66	1. **研究什么问题** — 1-2 句话交代背景和痛点
67	2. **为什么重要** — 现有方法的不足（不用写太多）
68	3. **本文提出什么** — 核心贡献的关键机制（简要，不堆砌细节）
69	4. **结果说明什么** — 用具体实验结论支撑贡献
70	
71	### 2.2 禁忌
72	
73	- **禁止堆砌数据**：Abstract 不是 Results 的缩写。最多引用 2-3 个关键数字，要服务于 claim
74	- **禁止过度宣传**：不说 "revolutionary"、"groundbreaking"
75	- **禁止写成 Introduction 的缩写**：Abstract 是独立阅读单元
76	- **禁止术语堆积**：如果审稿人几十秒内判断不出价值，就失败了
77	- **缩写必须定义**：Abstract 中首次出现的缩写必须展开
78	
79	### 2.3 审稿人视角检验
80	
81	写完后用这个标准自检：审稿人能否在 30 秒内判断——
82	- 问题是否重要？
83	- 方法是否清楚？
84	- 贡献是否可信？
85	- 结果是否有说服力？
86	
87	---
88	
89	## 三、Introduction 写作规范
90	
91	### 3.1 完整叙事线（5 段结构）
92	
93	Introduction 需要从一个清晰、重要的问题切入，逐步把读者带到核心贡献：
94	
95	1. **研究背景**（1-2 段）：大模型在某类能力/应用中已取得进展，但仍存在一个关键瓶颈
96	2. **问题重要性**（1 段）：结合真实应用、已有方法局限或当前趋势，说明这不是小问题
97	3. **现有方法不足**（1 段）：系统梳理为什么不够（依赖更大模型/更多数据/更复杂推理/更高成本，或只在特定 benchmark 有效）
98	4. **本文核心想法**（1 段）：我们观察到什么、提出了什么方法、为什么能解决上述问题
99	5. **贡献总结**（列表）：清楚列出方法贡献、实验贡献和分析贡献
100	
```

</details>

      "content": "---\nname: paper-writing-norms\ndescription: 顶会级别论文写作风格规范——章节写法（Abstract/Intro/Related Work）、去 AI 风格、视觉密度、叙事哲学。Paper Agent 和 Worker 写论文章节时必须加载。\nallowed-tools: [Read, Write, Edit, Glob, Grep]\nscope: global\nversion: 2\npriority: 9\ntags:\n  - writing\n  - paper\n  - style\n  - norms\nroles:\n  - agent\n  - worker\n  - paper_agent\n---\n\n# 论文写作风格规范\n\n> 核心哲学：**论文是一个有说服力的故事，不是实验报告。** 审稿人在几十秒内判断论文价值——如果 Abstract 和 Introduction 读起来像实验日志，论文就已经失败了。\n\n---\n\n## 一、总体写作哲学\n\n### 1.1 故事驱动，不是数据堆砌\n\n- 论文必须以\"故事线\"为核心展开：问题 → 洞察 → 方法 → 验证\n- 禁止大量实验数据堆砌——每个数字出现必须服务于叙事推进\n- 附录内容必须在正文中有明确引用且融入叙事，禁止简单堆砌\n- 如果附录数据对核心叙事有重大影响，根据附录数据调整论文 framing\n\n### 1.2 主线统一性\n\n- 当 idea 在研究过程中发生变化后，**必须重审全文逻辑**\n- 常见问题：Introduction 讲的故事 A，Method 做的是故事 B，Results 又在讲故事 C\n- 修复方式：从最终实验结论反推，重写 Introduction 的问题定义和动机\n- 每次 idea 调整后检查：Abstract → Introduction → Method → Results → Discussion → Conclusion 是否形成一条连贯主线\n\n### 1.3 专有名词必须有定义\n\n- 首次出现的专有名词/缩写必须给出完整定义和解释\n- Abstract 中使用的缩写也必须在 Abstract 内定义（如 LLM = Large Language Model）\n- 不能假设读者知道你自己定义的术语——论文从来没解释过的概念不能直接使用\n- 通用规则：如果一个非标准术语第一次出现时没有括号定义，就是写作 bug\n\n### 1.4 写作前必读同类优秀论文\n\n- 每个章节动笔前，找 **2-3 篇目标会议近 1-2 年的高分论文**作为风格参考——同类问题或同类方法皆可\n- 重点学：\n  - Abstract 怎么用最少篇幅讲完故事\n  - Introduction 怎么从大问题切入到具体贡献\n  - Related Work 怎么 positioning 自己（不是 survey）\n  - Table/Figure 怎么压缩信息密度\n- **不是抄袭**，是学习同行的故事节奏和审美——不同子领域叙事节奏差异很大\n- 实操：Paper Agent 可以从 `literature_map.yaml` 中挑 `role=foundation` 或 `role=direct-competitor` 的论文，把 `{title, venue, year, abstract}` 附在 Worker 的 task_prompt 中作为概念参考；如需学习写作风格本身，让 Worker 通过 WebSearch 取得论文链接并 fetch 全文\n\n---\n\n## 二、Abstract 写作规范\n\n### 2.1 四个必答问题（150-250 words）\n\nAbstract 必须在很短的篇幅内清楚回答：\n1. **研究什么问题** — 1-2 句话交代背景和痛点\n2. **为什么重要** — 现有方法的不足（不用写太多）\n3. **本文提出什么** — 核心贡献的关键机制（简要，不堆砌细节）\n4. **结果说明什么** — 用具体实验结论支撑贡献\n\n### 2.2 禁忌\n\n- **禁止堆砌数据**：Abstract 不是 Results 的缩写。最多引用 2-3 个关键数字，要服务于 claim\n- **禁止过度宣传**：不说 \"revolutionary\"、\"groundbreaking\"\n- **禁止写成 Introduction 的缩写**：Abstract 是独立阅读单元\n- **禁止术语堆积**：如果审稿人几十秒内判断不出价值，就失败了\n- **缩写必须定义**：Abstract 中首次出现的缩写必须展开\n\n### 2.3 审稿人视角检验\n\n写完后用这个标准自检：审稿人能否在 30 秒内判断——\n- 问题是否重要？\n- 方法是否清楚？\n- 贡献是否可信？\n- 结果是否有说服力？\n\n---\n\n## 三、Introduction 写作规范\n\n### 3.1 完整叙事线（5 段结构）\n\nIntroduction 需要从一个清晰、重要的问题切入，逐步把读者带到核心贡献：\n\n1. **研究背景**（1-2 段）：大模型在某类能力/应用中已取得进展，但仍存在一个关键瓶颈\n2. **问题重要性**（1 段）：结合真实应用、已有方法局限或当前趋势，说明这不是小问题\n3. **现有方法不足**（1 段）：系统梳理为什么不够（依赖更大模型/更多数据/更复杂推理/更高成本，或只在特定 benchmark 有效）\n4. **本文核心想法**（1 段）：我们观察到什么、提出了什么方法、为什么能解决上述问题\n5. **贡献总结**（列表）：清楚列出方法贡献、实验贡献和分析贡献\n",
      "numLines": 100,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 276
    }
  },

100	
101	### 3.2 整体叙事结构
102	
103	```
104	现有进展 → 关键缺口 → 本文洞察 → 方法概述 → 实验证据 → 贡献总结
105	```
106	
107	### 3.3 禁忌
108	
109	- **禁止泛泛介绍**：不要开头就写 "LLMs have shown remarkable capabilities..."，要从具体问题切入
110	- **禁止缺少过渡**：每段之间必须有逻辑连接
111	- **禁止实验报告风格**：Introduction 不是 "我们做了 X 实验得到了 Y 结果" 的罗列
112	- 每条 Contribution 必须有对应的 section/table 支撑
113	- Contribution 列表中不能出现未在前文铺垫过的概念
114	
115	---
116	
117	## 四、Related Work 写作规范
118	
119	### 4.1 目标
120	
121	让审稿人相信：作者理解这个领域，知道自己的方法站在什么位置，清楚相对已有工作的真实增量在哪里。
122	
123	### 4.2 结构要求
124	
125	- 把已有工作分成几个清晰的主题（如模型训练、推理增强、工具调用、数据构建、评测等）
126	- 每类简要说明：已有方法做了什么、解决了哪些问题、和本文的关系
127	- 帮助读者理解：哪些是本文直接继承的，哪些方法不同但解决相似问题，哪些留下了本文要解决的缺口
128	
129	### 4.3 禁忌
130	
131	- **禁止论文堆砌**：不要写成 "A proposed X. B proposed Y. C proposed Z." 的列表
132	- **目标不是展示读了多少论文**，而是做归纳和比较
133	- Contribution claim（"To our knowledge, no prior work..."）应放在 Introduction，不在 Related Work
134	
135	### 4.4 引用充足度
136	
137	- 引用必须覆盖：**直接相关方法、baseline 论文、评测 benchmark 论文、理论基础**
138	- 任何一类缺失，审稿人会立刻看出来调研不充分
139	- 引用密度参照目标会议近年录用论文的量级——明显偏少意味着 Related Work 调研不足
140	
141	---
142	
143	## 五、去 AI 写作风格（硬规则）
144	
145	以下模式是 AI 生成文本的典型标志，审稿人一眼能看出。**必须全部消除。**
146	
147	### 5.1 严禁破折号（最显著的 AI 痕迹）
148	
149	**这是 AI 写作最容易被识别的标志，审稿人最敏感。单列为最高优先级硬规则。**
150	
151	- 解释性 em-dash（—，U+2014）和 en-dash（–，U+2013）必须**全部删除**
152	- 替代方式：用逗号、括号或拆分为两个句子
153	- 强制检查命令：
154	  ```bash
155	  grep -n "—" paper/*.tex     # em-dash，每处人工判断是否必要
156	  grep -n " – " paper/*.tex   # 解释性 en-dash，几乎都应删除
157	  ```
158	- 例外（允许保留）：表格分隔符、数字范围（`2020–2024`、`pp. 12–18`）、专有名词（`Newton–Raphson`）
159	
160	### 5.2 段落与句式
161	
162	- **禁止过度分段**：不需要每 2-3 句就换段，自然段 5-8 句是正常的
163	- **减少关系从句**（which, that, who）：能用简洁直接的句子就不用从句
164	- **禁止 -ing 短语结尾**：如 "..., highlighting the applicability of..." / "..., demonstrating the effectiveness of..."。这是 AI 写作最典型的标志之一
165	- **禁止连续使用 "Furthermore" / "Moreover" / "Additionally"**：一段内最多用一个过渡词
166	- **避免 "It is worth noting that..." / "Notably, ..."**：直接写要说的内容
167	
168	### 5.3 用词黑名单
169	
170	- 避免 "delve into" / "leverage" / "utilize" / "facilitate" — 用 "explore" / "use" / "enable"
171	- 避免 "a myriad of" / "a plethora of" — 用 "many" / "various"
172	- 避免 "in the realm of" / "in the context of" — 直接说
173	- 避免 "paradigm shift" / "novel" 过度使用
174	
175	### 5.4 校对清单（4 步）
176	
177	1. 全文 proofread 语法和拼写
178	2. 检查所有代词/指示词（this, that, it）是否指代明确
179	3. **`grep "—"` + `grep " – "` 全文清零**（删除所有解释性 dash，§5.1）
180	4. `grep "ing,"` 模式——大部分 -ing 分词短语需要改写
181	
182	---
183	
184	## 六、图表与视觉规范
185	
186	### 6.1 知识密度原则
187	
188	- **图表的信息必须紧凑**——不能为了美观而留大量白空间
189	- 一张图如果只传达一个简单信息，要么缩小要么合并到其他图中
190	- 能用 inline text 说清的不要占一整个 Figure
191	
192	### 6.2 表格规范
193	
194	- 表格和正文文字不能视觉重叠（检查 column 宽度和 alignment）
195	- 小数精度全表统一
196	- 最优加粗、次优下划线
197	- Caption 必须自包含
198	
199	### 6.3 图片规范
````

Let me scan for specific issues:

**Introduction analysis:**
- Para 1 (lines 3-6): Good hook — "more information should help" but "this intuition is wrong". Clean, compelling.


- Para 2 (lines 8-10): "This approach aligns with recent *CL precedent" — good positioning. But the sentence structure is a bit listy.
- Para 3 (lines 12-13): Setup paragraph — fine.
- Para 4 (lines 15-17): Central result + regex + interesting question framing. Good.
- Para 5 (lines 19-21): Gradient masking + training dynamics + tail truncation. Three disparate findings crammed into one paragraph — could be tighter.
- Contribution list (lines 23-32): C1-C4, each with section refs.

**Issues found:**
1. Em-dashes: Line 19 has triple hyphens that should be proper em-dashes. Also the phrasing around "information overload" is unnecessarily wordy and sounds like AI-generated text — I should tighten that up.
2. Line 10: The "*CL" notation looks awkward; I should either clarify what I mean by recent precedent or just say "recent NLP work" to avoid the asterisk confusion.
3. Line 6: "despite containing strictly more information" is already concise and works well.
4. Need to review the contribution list C1-C4 for clarity and consistency.

Looking at the results section, C4 shows that gradient masking has minimal impact while content quality dominates the explanation gap — though I'm repeating the parenthetical about diagnostic signal density that I already mentioned earlier, so I should remove that redundancy. The Related Work section covers execution traces, code verdict classification, and patch correction work pretty thoroughly across the cited papers.

Now I'm checking the de-AI patterns in the LaTeX source, particularly looking for em-dashes and how they're formatted. I notice the intro uses em-dashes to set off the diagnostic signal density phrase, and the discussion section has similar patterns.

For the edits, I need to handle the double em-dash construction in the introduction—it's awkward and should be restructured with parentheses instead. The "*CL" notation on line 10 needs fixing too. The central result sits in lines 15-17, with supporting evidence following, so the flow is reasonable but the training dynamics sentence could be more concise. The related work section already has the IB, feature selection, and LIMA references properly cited in paragraph 4.

I should check for those AI markers systematically: the em-dashes (intro line 19 and discussion line 5), filler phrases like "Furthermore" or "notably," and "-ing," constructions. The phrase about compressing task-irrelevant information improving learning is solid—it connects theory to our actual findings.

Now I'll tackle the introduction line 19 rewrite. The current version tries to pack gradient masking ablation results, gradient dilution percentages, and content quality mechanisms into one sentence. I'll split this into clearer pieces and remove the em-dash construction entirely.

For the discussion line 5, I need to verify what's already been changed there since I worked on it earlier.

The `*CL` notation on line 10 is standard in NLP papers as shorthand for the ACL conference family, so I'll keep it as is.

Now I'm systematically going through the introduction to address em-dashes and tighten the language—starting with line 19, then checking lines 20 and the C1-C4 callouts for any unnecessary overlap with the surrounding paragraphs.

For the related work section, I've confirmed that information theory, feature selection, and LIMA are already covered, there are no obvious gaps, and I should do a final check for any AI-related language that needs adjusting.

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nExecution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \\citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.\nThe intuition is straightforward: more execution information should help the model make better predictions.\nYet for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), this intuition is wrong.\nComplete traces \\emph{actively degrade} accuracy compared to compact exception events, despite containing strictly more information.\n\nPrior work found trace utility to be task-dependent \\citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks where output noise confounds the analysis.\nVerdict prediction offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, and controlled component ablation directly measures how trace content affects SFT learning.\nThis approach aligns with recent *CL precedent: CRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval} uses execution prediction to probe code reasoning, and NExT \\citep{ni2024next} uses trace-augmented chain-of-thought for program repair.\n\nWe present a systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.\nUsing 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program}, we evaluate four models across three architecture families and 1.5B to 13B parameters: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \\citep{qwen2024coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \\citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \\citep{deepseek2024coder}.\n\nThe central result: complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all models and seeds (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\nA simple regex baseline (B5) that exploits the \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker matches this ceiling at 99.33\\%; even a trivial pattern suffices, so the interesting question is not why exceptions help but \\emph{why complete traces hurt}.\nMarker removal collapses B5 to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms that SFT acquires language-level diagnostic patterns beyond format matching (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\nA controlled gradient masking ablation limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the degradation; content quality---diagnostic signal density at inference, the mechanism underlying what is intuitively termed ``information overload''---accounts for ${\\geq}$95\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\nTraining dynamics confirm this: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates between 82--89\\% without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\nA tail-truncation budget sweep reveals a sharp performance jump at ${\\sim}$512 tokens; this threshold coincides with the globals module dump (${\\sim}$384 tokens), beyond which structured \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values appear (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Complete traces are strictly dominated by compact exception events}: across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}). Training dynamics directly show the mechanism: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching}: a regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms the learned signal generalizes (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{A two-tier signal hierarchy with a practical trace selection strategy}: Tier~1 (exceptions, assertions; ${\\geq}$98.53\\%) dominates Tier~2 (loop compression; 77--86\\%) by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs across all four models. A sharp performance transition at ${\\sim}$512 tail tokens yields a practical fallback: exception events first, tail-truncated state otherwise (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, \\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Controlled decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: gradient masking on \\texttt{exception\\_only} ($-$0.84pp under 99.8\\% mask) limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the 18pp gap; content quality (diagnostic signal density at inference) is the dominant factor at ${\\geq}$95\\%. A complementary label-only ablation on the Qwen family yields a directionally consistent but confounded upper bound (${\\leq}$26\\%) (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

{

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
