    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nExecution traces (line-by-line records of a program's runtime behavior) have emerged as a promising input modality for code language models \\citep{chen2025tracepile,ding2024traced,wang2025dcsh,ni2024next}.\nThe intuition is straightforward: more execution information should help the model make better predictions.\nYet for verdict prediction (classifying whether a test passes or fails), this intuition is wrong.\nComplete traces \\emph{actively degrade} accuracy compared to compact exception events, despite containing strictly more information.\n\nPrior work found trace utility to be task-dependent \\citep{wang2025dcsh}, but focused on generation-oriented tasks where output noise confounds the analysis.\nVerdict prediction offers a cleaner testbed: as binary classification, it isolates the learning signal from generation noise, and controlled component ablation directly measures how trace content affects SFT learning.\nThis approach aligns with recent *CL precedent: CRUXEval \\citep{gu2024cruxeval} uses execution prediction to probe code reasoning, and NExT \\citep{ni2024next} uses trace-augmented chain-of-thought for program repair.\n\nWe present a systematic component ablation of trace representations for verdict prediction.\nUsing 591 problems from HumanEval \\citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \\citep{austin2021program}, we evaluate four models across three architecture families and 1.5B to 13B parameters: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B/1.5B-Instruct \\citep{qwen2024coder}, CodeLlama-13B-Instruct \\citep{roziere2024code}, and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct \\citep{deepseek2024coder}.\n\nThe central result: complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% across all models and seeds (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}).\nA simple regex baseline (B5) that exploits the \\texttt{[No exception data in trace]} format marker matches this ceiling at 99.33\\%; even a trivial pattern suffices, so the interesting question is not why exceptions help but \\emph{why complete traces hurt}.\nMarker removal collapses B5 to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms that SFT acquires language-level diagnostic patterns beyond format matching (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\nA controlled gradient masking ablation limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the degradation; content quality (diagnostic signal density at inference) accounts for ${\\geq}$95\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\nTraining dynamics confirm this: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps, while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates between 82--89\\% without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\nA tail-truncation budget sweep reveals a sharp performance jump at ${\\sim}$512 tokens; this threshold coincides with the globals module dump (${\\sim}$384 tokens), beyond which structured \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values appear (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Complete traces are strictly dominated by compact exception events}: across four models (1.5B--13B, three families), complete traces (77--82\\%) barely improve over source code alone (${\\sim}$73--76\\%) while exception events achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}). Training dynamics directly show the mechanism: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates without stable convergence (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{SFT learns genuine diagnostic patterns beyond format matching}: a regex exploiting format markers matches the exception ceiling (99.33\\%), but marker removal collapses it to 28.49\\% while SFT retains 97.65\\% ($-$2.01pp); bidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) confirms the learned signal generalizes (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{A two-tier signal hierarchy with a practical trace selection strategy}: Tier~1 (exceptions, assertions; ${\\geq}$98.53\\%) dominates Tier~2 (loop compression; 77--86\\%) by 12+pp with non-overlapping CIs across all four models. A sharp performance transition at ${\\sim}$512 tail tokens yields a practical fallback: exception events first, tail-truncated state otherwise (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}, \\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Controlled decomposition of the full-trace degradation}: gradient masking on \\texttt{exception\\_only} ($-$0.84pp under 99.8\\% mask) limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the 18pp gap; content quality (diagnostic signal density at inference) is the dominant factor at ${\\geq}$95\\%. A complementary label-only ablation on the Qwen family yields a directionally consistent but confounded upper bound (${\\leq}$26\\%) (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
        "numLines": 33,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 33
      }
    },

{
  "parentUuid": "ca7c8b00-4a1a-4571-b0dc-d26b31736c64",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_4/projects/trace-verdict-study/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}\nThe results suggest a descriptive two-pathway framework---not a causal model---for why complete traces degrade SFT learning.\n\n\\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}\nThe diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\\S2.1) correlates with accuracy at $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$) across five conditions, and all four models achieve ${\\geq}$99.33\\% under \\texttt{exception\\_only} with zero variance on Qwen ($\\sigma{=}$0.00, 3 seeds).\nAlthough 55.5\\% of failures are silent logic errors, \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 100\\% fail recall because test assertions generate \\texttt{AssertionError} events.\n\\texttt{ae\\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms \\texttt{exception\\_only} (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that additional content introduces noise.\n\n\\paragraph{Implicit pathway: terminal execution state.}\nTail-truncated traces achieve 93--97\\% accuracy (Table~\\ref{tab:tail_cross_model}) despite $d {\\approx} 0$.\nTail truncation at ${\\geq}$512 tokens captures \\texttt{[Line~X]} variable values at final execution steps, which indirectly encode program outcome; below 384 tokens, the globals module dump consumes the entire budget, yielding ${\\sim}$73\\% ($\\approx$ \\texttt{no\\_trace}).\n\n\\paragraph{Diagnostic signal dilution in complete traces.}\nBoth pathways' signals exist in full traces but are buried under thousands of intermediate tokens.\nControlled gradient masking confirms this: $-$0.84pp under 99.8\\% mask limits gradient dilution to ${\\leq}$5\\% of the gap (\\S\\ref{sec:density}).\nFigure~\\ref{fig:training_dynamics} confirms: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges within 50 steps (std\\,=\\,0 by step 200), while \\texttt{full\\_trace} oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence (final std\\,=\\,0.014); eval accuracy CV differs 9.4$\\times$ (Appendix~\\ref{sec:training_dynamics}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{training_dynamics}\n\\caption{Training dynamics: \\texttt{exception\\_only} converges to 99.83\\% by step 150; \\texttt{full\\_trace} with label-only loss oscillates (82--89\\%) without convergence.}\n\\label{fig:training_dynamics}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Why SFT over regex?}\nSFT \\texttt{exception\\_only} (99.83\\%) exceeds the best regex (B5, 99.33\\%) by 0.50pp on caught-exception boundary cases (McNemar $p{=}.014$), provides calibrated probabilities (ECE\\,=\\,0.0027; \\S\\ref{sec:calibration}), and retains 97.65\\% under marker removal vs.\\ B5's 28.49\\% (\\S\\ref{sec:nomarker}).\nBidirectional cross-dataset transfer (99.4--100\\%) with near-majority \\texttt{no\\_trace} performance confirms the learned patterns are language-level, not dataset-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{The marginal value of complete traces.}\nSource code alone achieves 73--76\\% (\\texttt{no\\_trace}), so traces add at most ${\\sim}$27pp; complete traces capture only 4--9pp of this headroom while exception events capture 24--27pp.\nThat more information degrades performance aligns with feature selection theory \\citep{guyon2003introduction}, the Information Bottleneck \\citep{tishby2000information}, and LIMA \\citep{zhou2023lima}.\n\n\\paragraph{Design implications.}\nThe two-tier hierarchy suggests a practical fallback: extract exception events first; resort to tail-truncated state otherwise.\nTier~2 benefit is model-dependent (Qwen +10.9pp; CodeLlama +0.9pp), so trace design should match deployment model capacity.\n\n",
        "numLines": 40,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 40
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/discussion.tex"
  },

### Tool result

_source line 8324 · 2026-05-22T14:11:46.963Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CEq9kfz6qzNVVxUaBDcBZ1`</summary>

```text
130	The strongest semantic regex B4 (exception class matching, 93.61\%) is marker-invariant; SFT \texttt{nomarker} (97.65\%) exceeds it by 4.04pp, isolating the learned semantic contribution beyond pattern matching.
131	
132	\paragraph{Cross-dataset transfer.}
133	Bidirectional evaluation confirms generalization: HumanEval${\to}$MBPP \texttt{exception\_only} achieves 99.41\% ($N{=}4{,}270$) and MBPP${\to}$HumanEval achieves 100.0\% ($N{=}1{,}640$), while \texttt{no\_trace} drops to near-majority baseline in both directions (70.56\% vs.\ 69.02\% majority; 67.99\% vs.\ 61.65\%).
134	The near-zero transfer loss for exception signals---across independently authored problem sets with no problem overlap---confirms SFT learns language-level diagnostic patterns, not dataset-specific artifacts.
135	
136	\subsection{Decomposing the Full-Trace Degradation}
137	\label{sec:density}
138	
139	The 18.07pp gap between \texttt{exception\_only} (99.83\%) and \texttt{full\_trace} (81.76\%) could reflect gradient dilution (diagnostic tokens are ${\sim}$1/500 of full traces, diluting gradient signal during training) or content quality (verbose input buries diagnostic signals at inference).
140	
141	\paragraph{Controlled gradient masking.}
142	To isolate gradient dilution, we mask 99.8\% of gradient updates during \texttt{exception\_only} training, matching the diagnostic token ratio of full traces while preserving compact input.
143	This yields 98.99\% ($-$0.84pp from \texttt{exception\_only}), limiting gradient dilution to ${\leq}$5\% of the 18.07pp gap (Table~\ref{tab:density}).
144	Content quality---diagnostic signal density at inference---accounts for the remaining ${\geq}$95\%.
145	Note that masking affects only the backward pass; the model still attends to all input tokens during the forward pass, so this bounds gradient dilution's contribution but does not rule out attention-based information flow as a residual confound.
146	
147	\begin{table}[t]
148	\centering
149	\caption{Decomposition of the full-trace degradation (Qwen-7B). Gradient masking isolates gradient dilution to ${\leq}$5\%; content quality (signal density at inference) accounts for ${\geq}$95\%. Label-only loss provides a confounded but directionally consistent complementary estimate.}
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 8325 · 2026-05-22T14:11:48.432Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01M4uHAPFFEGePzTcWvej91c`</summary>

```text
48	To probe the contribution of gradient dilution, we introduce \texttt{full\_trace\_label\_only}: identical full-trace input but loss computed only on the verdict token (trace positions masked).
49	TRL's SFTTrainer computes mean cross-entropy over label tokens, so label-only loss does not amplify the effective learning rate.
50	However, this ablation simultaneously changes three variables: (1)~which tokens receive gradient updates, (2)~the implicit regularization from predicting trace tokens, and (3)~the training signal distribution.
51	The resulting improvement over standard \texttt{full\_trace} provides a confounded upper bound on gradient dilution's contribution.
52	A cleaner test---masking 99.8\% of gradient updates during \texttt{exception\_only} training---matches the diagnostic token ratio of full traces while preserving compact input, isolating gradient dilution from content quality (\S\ref{sec:density}).
53	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{A descriptive two-pathway framework.}
5	The results suggest a descriptive two-pathway framework---not a causal model---for why complete traces degrade SFT learning.
6	
7	\paragraph{Explicit pathway: diagnostic tokens.}
8	The diagnostic token ratio $d$ (\S2.1) correlates with accuracy at $r{=}0.949$ ($p{=}0.014$) across five conditions, and all four models achieve ${\geq}$99.33\% under \texttt{exception\_only} with zero variance on Qwen ($\sigma{=}$0.00, 3 seeds).
9	Although 55.5\% of failures are silent logic errors, \texttt{exception\_only} achieves 100\% fail recall because test assertions generate \texttt{AssertionError} events.
10	\texttt{ae\_only}, a strict superset, slightly underperforms \texttt{exception\_only} (Table~\ref{tab:ablation}), confirming that additional content introduces noise.
```

</details>
